### DRC: Enhancing Personalized Image Generation via Disentangled Representation Composition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17349v1)
- **Authors**: Yiyan Xu, Wuqiang Zheng, Wenjie Wang, Fengbin Zhu, Xinting Hu, Yang Zhang, Fuli Feng, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Personalized image generation has emerged as a promising direction in multimodal content creation. It aims to synthesize images tailored to individual style preferences (e.g., color schemes, character appearances, layout) and semantic intentions (e.g., emotion, action, scene contexts) by leveraging user-interacted history images and multimodal instructions. Despite notable progress, existing methods -- whether based on diffusion models, large language models, or Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) -- struggle to accurately capture and fuse user style preferences and semantic intentions. In particular, the state-of-the-art LMM-based method suffers from the entanglement of visual features, leading to Guidance Collapse, where the generated images fail to preserve user-preferred styles or reflect the specified semantics. To address these limitations, we introduce DRC, a novel personalized image generation framework that enhances LMMs through Disentangled Representation Composition. DRC explicitly extracts user style preferences and semantic intentions from history images and the reference image, respectively, to form user-specific latent instructions that guide image generation within LMMs. Specifically, it involves two critical learning stages: 1) Disentanglement learning, which employs a dual-tower disentangler to explicitly separate style and semantic features, optimized via a reconstruction-driven paradigm with difficulty-aware importance sampling; and 2) Personalized modeling, which applies semantic-preserving augmentations to effectively adapt the disentangled representations for robust personalized generation. Extensive experiments on two benchmarks demonstrate that DRC shows competitive performance while effectively mitigating the guidance collapse issue, underscoring the importance of disentangled representation learning for controllable and effective personalized image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DRC, a novel framework aimed at enhancing personalized image generation by addressing the "Guidance Collapse" issue prevalent in Large Multimodal Model (LMM)-based approaches. Guidance Collapse occurs when LMMs fail to accurately capture and fuse user style preferences from history images with semantic intentions from reference images. DRC tackles this by employing a two-stage process: 1) Disentanglement Learning, which uses a dual-tower architecture to explicitly separate style and semantic features via a reconstruction-driven paradigm with difficulty-aware importance sampling; and 2) Personalized Modeling, which adapts the disentangled representations for robust personalized generation using semantic-preserving augmentations. Extensive experiments on sticker and movie poster generation scenarios demonstrate that DRC exhibits competitive performance while mitigating guidance collapse.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit disentanglement of style and semantics within an LMM framework for personalized image generation. While disentanglement is a well-established concept, its application within the specific context of personalized image generation using LMMs, along with the difficulty-aware importance sampling and semantic-preserving augmentations, is a unique contribution. The architecture addresses a specific shortcoming of existing LMM-based personalization methods.

*   **Significance:** Addressing guidance collapse is a significant step towards achieving truly controllable and effective personalized image generation. The experimental results demonstrate the practical effectiveness of DRC, indicating its potential to improve the quality and relevance of generated images. This has direct implications for real-world applications such as personalized advertising, content creation platforms, and user interface customization.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a key limitation in existing LMM-based personalized image generation.
    *   The proposed DRC framework is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   The dual-tower disentangler with difficulty-aware importance sampling is an innovative approach.
    *   Semantic-preserving augmentations are a clever method for improving robustness.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering two distinct scenarios and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   Both quantitative and qualitative results support the effectiveness of DRC.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LaVIT as a backbone might limit the generalizability of the findings to other LMM architectures. Though the authors state its compatibility, empirical validation across several LMMs is missing.
    *   The specific choices of loss functions and network architectures within the dual-tower disentangler could benefit from further ablation studies to determine optimal configurations. A wider parameter search may prove to be useful.
    *   While the paper mentions that the code is publicly available, actually accessing and verifying reproducibility will require additional review.
    * The evaluation metrics, while following existing methods, are somewhat subjective and can be improved with more objective comparisons between generated images and existing history/reference images. User studies are needed to complement the quantitative evaluation and assess the perceptual quality.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The DRC framework has the potential to influence future research in personalized image generation by highlighting the importance of disentangled representations and providing a practical solution for mitigating guidance collapse.  It is likely to stimulate further exploration of disentanglement techniques within LMMs and may inspire new architectural designs for personalized content creation. The specific components of DRC, like the difficulty-aware sampling, can be reused in other related problems.

**Justification:** The paper presents a well-defined solution to a concrete problem in personalized image generation, and the design includes several novel components for disentanglement and the application for the use-case. While some aspects could benefit from further refinement and broader validation, the core idea is solid and has strong practical relevance. The work presents interesting architectural choices which are potentially reusable in other contexts.

### PatientDx: Merging Large Language Models for Protecting Data-Privacy in Healthcare
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17360v1)
- **Authors**: Jose G. Moreno, Jesus Lovon, M'Rick Robin-Charlet, Christine Damase-Michel, Lynda Tamine
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) has become the default practice for improving model performance on a given task. However, performance improvement comes at the cost of training on vast amounts of annotated data which could be sensitive leading to significant data privacy concerns. In particular, the healthcare domain is one of the most sensitive domains exposed to data privacy issues. In this paper, we present PatientDx, a framework of model merging that allows the design of effective LLMs for health-predictive tasks without requiring fine-tuning nor adaptation on patient data. Our proposal is based on recently proposed techniques known as merging of LLMs and aims to optimize a building block merging strategy. PatientDx uses a pivotal model adapted to numerical reasoning and tunes hyperparameters on examples based on a performance metric but without training of the LLM on these data. Experiments using the mortality tasks of the MIMIC-IV dataset show improvements up to 7% in terms of AUROC when compared to initial models. Additionally, we confirm that when compared to fine-tuned models, our proposal is less prone to data leak problems without hurting performance. Finally, we qualitatively show the capabilities of our proposal through a case study. Our best model is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/ Jgmorenof/mistral\_merged\_0\_4.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PatientDx: Merging Large Language Models for Protecting Data-Privacy in Healthcare":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PatientDx, a framework for building effective Large Language Models (LLMs) for healthcare predictive tasks while mitigating data privacy concerns.  The core idea is to merge pre-trained LLMs, leveraging techniques like Model Soup and SLerp, instead of fine-tuning on sensitive patient data. PatientDx employs a pivotal model adapted for numerical reasoning and optimizes hyperparameters on a performance metric, avoiding direct training on private data. Experiments on the MIMIC-IV dataset demonstrate improvements in AUROC compared to initial models and reduced susceptibility to data leakage compared to fine-tuned models. The paper also provides a qualitative case study highlighting the model's capabilities and transferability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using model merging for privacy-preserving LLMs in healthcare is somewhat novel, although model merging itself is a well-established technique. Applying this specifically to healthcare and focusing on tasks relying on numerical reasoning in EHR data adds a layer of contribution. The optimization of the merging strategy without direct fine-tuning on patient data is the key differentiating factor.
*   **Significance:** Healthcare is a domain where data privacy is paramount. The paper addresses a crucial need by offering an alternative to fine-tuning, which often involves high privacy risks. If PatientDx proves effective and generalizable, it could potentially reduce the reliance on sensitive data for model development in healthcare, which could have substantial practical implications.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear motivation rooted in the privacy challenges of using LLMs in healthcare.
    *   The use of model merging as a privacy-preserving technique is well-explained.
    *   The experimental setup using MIMIC-IV is appropriate and the choice of mortality prediction tasks is relevant.
    *   The comparison with fine-tuned models and evaluation of data leakage using DLT metrics strengthens the claims.
    *   The qualitative case study offers insights into the model's capabilities beyond simple metrics.
    *   The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of individual components of the framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental results, while positive, could benefit from more extensive evaluation across a wider range of healthcare tasks and datasets.  Only mortality prediction in MIMIC-IV is used.
    *   The hyperparameter optimization process, while described, could be elaborated further. The choice of k-fold validation for hyperparameter tuning on a public dataset does not necessarily ensure optimal performance on real-world private data.
    *   The transferability evaluation is limited to a single qualitative case study and a task using BM25 for Information retrieval, which may not be enough to fully assess the generalizability of the approach.
    *   The paper claims reduced susceptibility to data leakage, but the DLT metrics are only indirect measures. Direct privacy attacks such as membership inference could provide stronger evidence.
    *   The focus on mortality tasks may limit the generality of conclusions since these tasks are heavily studied and often rely on specific data representations. The value and generalizability to other, more complex tasks requiring more context understanding may be limited.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the intersection of LLMs, healthcare, and data privacy. While the experimental validation could be strengthened with more diverse datasets and tasks, the core idea of using model merging to avoid direct fine-tuning on sensitive data is promising. The paper provides evidence for both improved performance and reduced data leakage risk, although more rigorous privacy evaluations are needed. The novelty is incremental but relevant to the field. The qualitative analysis shows potential for more complex reasoning, but more quantitative analyses would make the claims stronger.

### TimeSoccer: An End-to-End Multimodal Large Language Model for Soccer Commentary Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17365v1)
- **Authors**: Ling You, Wenxuan Huang, Xinni Xie, Xiangyi Wei, Bangyan Li, Shaohui Lin, Yang Li, Changbo Wang
- **Abstract**: Soccer is a globally popular sporting event, typically characterized by long matches and distinctive highlight moments. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer promising capabilities in temporal grounding and video understanding, soccer commentary generation often requires precise temporal localization and semantically rich descriptions over long-form video. However, existing soccer MLLMs often rely on the temporal a priori for caption generation, so they cannot process the soccer video end-to-end. While some traditional approaches follow a two-step paradigm that is complex and fails to capture the global context to achieve suboptimal performance. To solve the above issues, we present TimeSoccer, the first end-to-end soccer MLLM for Single-anchor Dense Video Captioning (SDVC) in full-match soccer videos. TimeSoccer jointly predicts timestamps and generates captions in a single pass, enabling global context modeling across 45-minute matches. To support long video understanding of soccer matches, we introduce MoFA-Select, a training-free, motion-aware frame compression module that adaptively selects representative frames via a coarse-to-fine strategy, and incorporates complementary training paradigms to strengthen the model's ability to handle long temporal sequences. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our TimeSoccer achieves State-of-The-Art (SoTA) performance on the SDVC task in an end-to-end form, generating high-quality commentary with accurate temporal alignment and strong semantic relevance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "TimeSoccer: An End-to-End Multimodal Large Language Model for Soccer Commentary Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TimeSoccer, a novel end-to-end multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed specifically for generating soccer commentary.  It addresses limitations in existing approaches that either rely on pre-defined timestamps or employ a two-stage process (segmentation then captioning). TimeSoccer jointly predicts timestamps and generates captions in a single pass, enabling it to capture the global context of a 45-minute soccer match. Key innovations include:

1.  **End-to-End SDVC:** A shift from two-stage methods to a single-pass approach for single-anchor dense video captioning (SDVC).
2.  **MoFA-Select:** A training-free, motion-aware frame compression module that adaptively selects representative frames, balancing semantic importance and motion dynamics. This helps manage the long video sequences without excessive computational cost.
3.  **Progressive Training and Positional Encoding Extrapolation:**  Strategies to support long-range temporal modeling, enabling the model to understand the entire 45-minute match.

The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on the SoccerNet-Caption dataset, showcasing improvements in both temporal localization and caption quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The end-to-end approach to SDVC in the context of soccer commentary is a significant contribution. Existing methods often decouple timestamp prediction from caption generation, hindering global context understanding. This joint prediction is a crucial advancement. The MoFA-Select module also presents a novel approach to frame selection, demonstrating a good balance between efficiency and preserving vital information.
*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a challenging and practical problem: generating high-quality commentary for soccer matches.  The demonstrated improvements in temporal grounding and caption quality can potentially impact real-world applications in sports broadcasting and content creation. Furthermore, the insights into long-form video understanding and MLLM architectures can be useful in other domains.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed, comparing TimeSoccer against strong baselines, including recent video MLLMs and soccer-specific methods. The use of both automatic metrics and human evaluation (via Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct) strengthens the validity of the results. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The figures are informative, and the method is explained in detail.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on Large Language Models:** The system relies heavily on pre-trained LLMs (LLaMA-2 and a vision transformer). While this is common, it limits the novelty to the architecture and training strategies specific to this application rather than significant advances in LLM technology itself. The "TimeChat" work it builds upon also falls into this category.
*   **Generalizability Concerns:** The model is specifically tailored to soccer. While the end-to-end framework and MoFA-Select module *might* be adaptable to other long-form video domains, the paper provides no evidence of this.  The soccer-specific knowledge embedded during training could limit its usefulness elsewhere.
*   **Limited Human Evaluation:** While the paper introduces an evaluation method using LLM itself, it would be beneficial to include human-based evaluation focusing on aspects such as the *engagement* and *enjoyment* that TimeSoccer's commentary can offer.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing an end-to-end architecture for soccer commentary generation, demonstrating significant improvements in both temporal localization and caption quality. The MoFA-Select module addresses the challenge of long-form video understanding in a computationally efficient manner. The paper's strong experimental results and comprehensive evaluation further solidify its significance.

However, it is important to acknowledge the reliance on existing large language models and the limited generalizability beyond the soccer domain. The dependency on LLMs like LLaMA-2 and Qwen reduces the focus on innovations within LLM technology itself.

Considering these strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7.5** is justified. The work is innovative in its architecture and demonstrates practical value in the specific domain of soccer commentary.  However, the heavy reliance on existing LLMs and the lack of generalizability studies prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### LiveLongBench: Tackling Long-Context Understanding for Spoken Texts from Live Streams
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17366v1)
- **Authors**: Yongxuan Wu, Runyu Chen, Peiyu Liu, Hongjin Qian
- **Abstract**: Long-context understanding poses significant challenges in natural language processing, particularly for real-world dialogues characterized by speech-based elements, high redundancy, and uneven information density. Although large language models (LLMs) achieve impressive results on existing benchmarks, these datasets fail to reflect the complexities of such texts, limiting their applicability to practical scenarios. To bridge this gap, we construct the first spoken long-text dataset, derived from live streams, designed to reflect the redundancy-rich and conversational nature of real-world scenarios. We construct tasks in three categories: retrieval-dependent, reasoning-dependent, and hybrid. We then evaluate both popular LLMs and specialized methods to assess their ability to understand long-contexts in these tasks. Our results show that current methods exhibit strong task-specific preferences and perform poorly on highly redundant inputs, with no single method consistently outperforming others. We propose a new baseline that better handles redundancy in spoken text and achieves strong performance across tasks. Our findings highlight key limitations of current methods and suggest future directions for improving long-context understanding. Finally, our benchmark fills a gap in evaluating long-context spoken language understanding and provides a practical foundation for developing real-world e-commerce systems. The code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Yarayx/livelongbench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LiveLongBench: Tackling Long-Context Understanding for Spoken Texts from Live Streams":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LiveLongBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate long-context understanding in spoken texts derived from live streams. The authors argue that existing long-context benchmarks primarily focus on formal written texts, neglecting the unique characteristics of spoken language, such as high redundancy, informal expressions, and topic drift.  LiveLongBench comprises a dataset of live stream transcripts annotated for a variety of tasks categorized into retrieval, reasoning, and hybrid types.  The authors evaluate several popular LLMs and context compression techniques on the benchmark, revealing limitations in their ability to handle the challenges posed by spoken language. They also propose a hybrid KV cache compression strategy that combines multiple techniques to achieve better performance-memory trade-offs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

*   **Focus on Spoken Language:**  The primary strength is addressing a gap in the existing benchmark landscape by focusing on long-context spoken texts. This is particularly relevant given the increasing prevalence of spoken language data in real-world applications.
*   **Dataset Construction:** The construction of a live stream dataset is significant because such data captures the authentic challenges of spoken language understanding. Recording from live streams, a rapidly evolving field, provides a unique perspective that contrasts with traditional, more structured corpora.
*   **Semantic Multi-Span Tasks:** Introduction of semantic multi-span reasoning is an important advancement. This addition forces the model to reason about text not grouped together, creating an increased level of difficulty and a more real-world usage scenario.
*   **Hybrid Compression Strategy:** The exploration and evaluation of hybrid KV cache compression strategies, informed by Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA), contribute new insights into optimizing performance-memory trade-offs.

**Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field in several ways:

*   **Driving Research:** The benchmark will likely stimulate research into developing more robust and efficient methods for processing spoken language data in long-context scenarios.
*   **Informing LLM Development:** The evaluation results can guide the development of LLMs that are better equipped to handle the specific challenges of spoken language, such as redundancy and informality.
*   **Practical Applications:** The findings related to context compression can lead to more efficient deployment of LLMs in real-world applications, especially those involving live stream data. By targeting key issues with the data and proposing a solution, the authors have created a useful tool.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a relevant and under-addressed problem in the field.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a comprehensive evaluation of various models and techniques on the benchmark.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.
*   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper offers practical recommendations for improving long-context understanding in spoken language.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Bias:** The reliance on Douyin e-commerce live streams introduces a specific bias, limiting the generalizability of the benchmark to other domains of spoken language. The evaluation section clearly focuses on the challenges the dataset presents but more discussion of potential biases would be appreciated.
*   **Limited Scope of Compression Methods:** The evaluation could benefit from an expanded scope of compression methods, especially those focusing on redundancy reduction or token selection tailored for spoken language.
*   **Annotation Cost:** As the authors acknowledge, the high cost of annotating live stream data is a limitation, potentially affecting the scale and diversity of the benchmark.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis**: While the empirical evaluation is strong, the paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why certain compression methods perform better in specific scenarios.

**Potential Influence:** The benchmark could become a standard for evaluating long-context understanding in spoken language, driving future research and development in the field. It can also inform the design of more efficient and robust LLMs for real-world applications.

**Overall Score Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing an important gap in existing benchmarks and providing a comprehensive evaluation of various approaches. While the dataset's domain-specific nature and the limited scope of compression methods are weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The research will have influence and likely guide development and research for years to come.

### On-Device Qwen2.5: Efficient LLM Inference with Model Compression and Hardware Acceleration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17376v1)
- **Authors**: Maoyang Xiang, Ramesh Fernando, Bo Wang
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced AI capabilities but pose considerable challenges for deployment on edge devices due to high computational demands, memory bandwidth constraints, and energy consumption. This paper addresses these challenges by presenting an efficient framework for deploying the Qwen2.5-0.5B model on the Xilinx Kria KV260 edge platform, a heterogeneous system integrating an ARM Cortex-A53 CPU with reconfigurable FPGA logic. Leveraging Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) with FPGA-accelerated execution pipelines, the proposed approach enhances both model compression rate and system throughput. Additionally, we propose a hybrid execution strategy that intelligently offloads compute-intensive operations to the FPGA while utilizing the CPU for lighter tasks, effectively balancing the computational workload and maximizing overall performance. Our framework achieves a model compression rate of 55.08% compared to the original model and produces output at a rate of 5.1 tokens per second, outperforming the baseline performance of 2.8 tokens per second.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for efficiently deploying the Qwen2.5-0.5B large language model on the Xilinx Kria KV260 edge platform. The framework leverages Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) for model compression and utilizes an FPGA-accelerated execution pipeline. A hybrid execution strategy is proposed, offloading compute-intensive operations to the FPGA while lighter tasks are handled by the CPU. The approach claims to achieve a 55.08% model compression rate and improves inference speed to 5.1 tokens per second, outperforming the baseline performance of 2.8 tokens per second.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a very important and timely challenge: deploying LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices.  This has significant implications for real-time AI applications and privacy-preserving computation.
*   **System-Level Optimization:** The paper combines software-level model compression (AWQ) with hardware acceleration (FPGA). This co-optimization approach is crucial for achieving good performance on edge devices.
*   **Specific Platform Focus:** Targeting the Xilinx Kria KV260 provides concrete implementation details, allowing for reproducibility and comparison against other works.
*   **Performance Improvement:**  The reported improvement in tokens per second compared to the baseline is significant and demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the key challenges in deploying LLMs on edge devices, namely memory constraints and computational bottlenecks associated with matrix multiplications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:**  AWQ is an existing model compression technique.  While the *application* of AWQ to Qwen2.5 and its integration with FPGA acceleration is novel, the *core* compression algorithm isn't new. Similarly, using FPGAs for accelerating matrix multiplications is well-established.
*   **Lack of Comparative Evaluation Against Other Acceleration Techniques:**  The paper focuses on FPGA acceleration but doesn't compare its performance against other edge deployment strategies, such as using optimized CPU/GPU libraries (e.g., TensorRT, OpenVINO), or other hardware acceleration techniques (e.g., specialized AI accelerators).
*   **Accuracy Drop:** The approach results in a slight decrease in accuracy (64.79% to 61.97%). The paper doesn't deeply analyze the cause of the accuracy drop or discuss strategies to mitigate it further. While the drop might be acceptable for some applications, a more in-depth analysis is warranted.
*   **Limited Discussion of Power Consumption:**  While efficiency is mentioned, the paper lacks detailed power consumption measurements. Edge deployment is heavily constrained by power budgets, and demonstrating the power efficiency of the approach is crucial.
*   **Simplistic Benchmark Score:** The benchmark scoring metric provided is not rigorous and doesn't appear to be sufficiently sophisticated to capture the relevant trade-offs between accuracy, latency, and resource usage, which are all key considerations for deployment.
*   **Resource Utilization limitations**  The paper reports 82% LUT utilization and 30% DSP utilization, suggesting headroom for more complex DSP-oriented logic designs.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating a practical approach to deploying a reasonably sized LLM (Qwen2.5-0.5B) on a commercially available edge device.  The work provides a good starting point for researchers and practitioners interested in edge-based LLM inference. However, the lack of detailed comparative evaluations against other techniques and limited power analysis somewhat limit the significance of the work.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has practical value and demonstrates a successful integration of existing techniques for a specific problem. However, the limited novelty in the individual components, lack of a comprehensive comparative analysis, accuracy drop without deep analysis, and limited discussion of power consumption prevent it from receiving a higher score.

### Assessing the Capability of Large Language Models for Domain-Specific Ontology Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17402v1)
- **Authors**: Anna Sofia Lippolis, Mohammad Javad Saeedizade, Robin Keskisarkka, Aldo Gangemi, Eva Blomqvist, Andrea Giovanni Nuzzolese
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown significant potential for ontology engineering. However, it is still unclear to what extent they are applicable to the task of domain-specific ontology generation. In this study, we explore the application of LLMs for automated ontology generation and evaluate their performance across different domains. Specifically, we investigate the generalizability of two state-of-the-art LLMs, DeepSeek and o1-preview, both equipped with reasoning capabilities, by generating ontologies from a set of competency questions (CQs) and related user stories. Our experimental setup comprises six distinct domains carried out in existing ontology engineering projects and a total of 95 curated CQs designed to test the models' reasoning for ontology engineering. Our findings show that with both LLMs, the performance of the experiments is remarkably consistent across all domains, indicating that these methods are capable of generalizing ontology generation tasks irrespective of the domain. These results highlight the potential of LLM-based approaches in achieving scalable and domain-agnostic ontology construction and lay the groundwork for further research into enhancing automated reasoning and knowledge representation techniques.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Assessing the Capability of Large Language Models for Domain-Specific Ontology Generation" explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for automatically generating ontologies from competency questions (CQs) and user stories. The authors evaluate two state-of-the-art LLMs, DeepSeek and OpenAI's o1-preview, across six distinct domains. The research aims to assess the generalizability of these LLMs in generating ontologies regardless of the domain. The authors create a dataset of 95 curated CQs and user stories from existing ontology engineering projects and evaluate the generated ontologies by manually annotating the LLM outputs. The study finds that both LLMs perform consistently across different domains, suggesting their potential for scalable and domain-agnostic ontology construction. The paper highlights the potential of LLMs in automating key aspects of ontology engineering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper presents a well-defined methodology with a manually annotated dataset. The choice of evaluating across six different domains is commendable, as it provides a more comprehensive assessment of generalizability than studies focused on single domains.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The paper includes a comparative analysis of two state-of-the-art LLMs (DeepSeek and OpenAI's o1-preview) with reasoning capabilities, offering insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses in ontology generation.
    *   **Clear and Focused Research Question:** The research question is clearly stated and relevant to the ongoing efforts in automating ontology engineering. The paper addresses a gap in the literature by focusing on the domain-agnostic performance of LLMs for ontology generation.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis**: The error analysis offers valuable insights into the type and distribution of errors produced by each model and proposes possible improvement strategies.
    *   **Well-structured and clearly written:** The paper follows a conventional research format and is clear and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of LLMs:** The study only evaluates two LLMs. While they are state-of-the-art, expanding the evaluation to include other relevant models (e.g., Flan-T5 variants, Llama family models) could provide a more comprehensive understanding of the landscape.
    *   **Subjectivity in Annotation:** The evaluation relies on manual annotation, which can introduce subjectivity. While the authors mention having two ontology engineers involved, further details on inter-annotator agreement or a more formal evaluation metric (e.g., precision, recall, F1-score against a gold standard ontology, if available) would strengthen the analysis. The definition of modeled, not modeled and minor issues can also be more rigorous.
    *   **Limited Depth of Ontology Evaluation:** The evaluation method primarily focuses on whether the CQs can be answered by the generated ontologies. This might not fully capture the quality and completeness of the ontology in terms of representing domain knowledge. Additional evaluation metrics (e.g., coherence, consistency, complexity, information content) would improve the assessment.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While 95 CQs is a reasonable start, a larger dataset could provide more robust statistical results, particularly when analyzing performance across different domains.
    *   **Risk of Dataset Leakage** This may impact the quality and reliability of the LLM-generated ontology

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty lies in the systematic evaluation of LLMs with reasoning capabilities for domain-agnostic ontology generation using competency questions. Prior work had often focused on specific domains or datasets. The significance is in demonstrating that LLMs can indeed be applied to ontology engineering across different domains.
    It contributes to the field by demonstrating that LLMs can generalize effectively across diverse domains, showing potential for automating ontology construction.
    The analysis of different error types provides practical guidance for improving future ontology generation systems and the models themselves.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    This paper can influence future research by encouraging further exploration of LLM-based ontology generation techniques. The findings can inform the development of more robust and generalizable ontology engineering tools. It can also lead to further investigation into prompt engineering techniques and the selection of suitable LLMs for ontology creation tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a useful evaluation of LLMs for ontology generation across multiple domains, but it has some limitations (scope of LLMs, and subjectivity in annotation). Therefore, a score of 7 is a good rating for the work considering its quality, contribution to the field, and potential for future influence.

### 3DV-TON: Textured 3D-Guided Consistent Video Try-on via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17414v1)
- **Authors**: Min Wei, Chaohui Yu, Jingkai Zhou, Fan Wang
- **Abstract**: Video try-on replaces clothing in videos with target garments. Existing methods struggle to generate high-quality and temporally consistent results when handling complex clothing patterns and diverse body poses. We present 3DV-TON, a novel diffusion-based framework for generating high-fidelity and temporally consistent video try-on results. Our approach employs generated animatable textured 3D meshes as explicit frame-level guidance, alleviating the issue of models over-focusing on appearance fidelity at the expanse of motion coherence. This is achieved by enabling direct reference to consistent garment texture movements throughout video sequences. The proposed method features an adaptive pipeline for generating dynamic 3D guidance: (1) selecting a keyframe for initial 2D image try-on, followed by (2) reconstructing and animating a textured 3D mesh synchronized with original video poses. We further introduce a robust rectangular masking strategy that successfully mitigates artifact propagation caused by leaking clothing information during dynamic human and garment movements. To advance video try-on research, we introduce HR-VVT, a high-resolution benchmark dataset containing 130 videos with diverse clothing types and scenarios. Quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate our superior performance over existing methods. The project page is at this link https://2y7c3.github.io/3DV-TON/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "3DV-TON: Textured 3D-Guided Consistent Video Try-on via Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper presents 3DV-TON, a novel framework for video try-on that leverages diffusion models and textured 3D guidance to achieve high-fidelity and temporally consistent results.  The core idea is to use animatable textured 3D meshes as explicit frame-level guidance, addressing the appearance-biased optimization problem which leads to motion incoherence in video diffusion models. This is accomplished through a pipeline that selects a keyframe for 2D try-on, reconstructs and animates a textured 3D mesh synchronized with the original video poses, and introduces a robust rectangular masking strategy.  The paper also introduces a high-resolution video try-on benchmark dataset, HR-VVT, and demonstrates through quantitative and qualitative results that 3DV-TON outperforms existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a key limitation:** The paper directly tackles a well-recognized limitation of video diffusion models: the tendency to prioritize appearance fidelity at the expense of temporal coherence. The explicit 3D guidance is a clever way to address this.
    *   **Novel use of 3D guidance:**  While some prior work uses 3D human priors, this paper's innovation lies in using _textured_ 3D meshes. This allows the framework to directly reference consistent garment texture movements, which is crucial for realistic video try-on.
    *   **Adaptive 3D guidance pipeline:** The proposed pipeline for generating dynamic 3D guidance including keyframe selection, textured 3D mesh reconstruction and animation synchronized with original video is adaptive and contributes for better results.
    *   **HR-VVT dataset:** The introduction of the HR-VVT dataset is a significant contribution, providing a much-needed high-resolution benchmark for evaluating video try-on methods in more diverse and complex scenarios.
    *   **Strong experimental results:**  The quantitative and qualitative results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of 3DV-TON over existing methods, especially in maintaining texture consistency and visual quality. The ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of the proposed 3D guidance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational complexity:** The use of 3D reconstruction adds a computational overhead. While the paper mentions a reduced reconstruction time through optimized objectives, the process is still likely more computationally intensive than methods relying solely on 2D techniques. Though, there is work that reduces the reconstruction time using feed-forward approach, the authors could explore it.
    *   **Reliance on external tools:** The pipeline depends on the accuracy of multiple external tools, including pose estimation, 3D reconstruction, and image try-on methods.  Errors or limitations in these tools could propagate through the pipeline and affect the final results.
    *   **Masking strategy:** The use of rectangular masking, while robust, might lead to some loss of detail or unnatural transitions, especially in areas with complex clothing deformations or occlusions. While the paper mentions an effort to counter excessive masking, this area could be further improved.
    *   **Limited scope:** The paper focuses on video try-on of clothes. It doesn't explore the generalizability of the approach to other types of video editing or content generation tasks.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty of the paper is in the specific combination of diffusion models with textured 3D guidance for video try-on. While diffusion models and 3D human models have been used previously, the explicit use of textured 3D meshes for consistent texture movement and the design of an adaptive 3D guidance pipeline are significant contributions. The HR-VVT dataset also provides a valuable resource for future research.

The significance of the paper lies in its ability to address a fundamental limitation of video diffusion models (temporal incoherence) and in pushing the boundaries of video try-on technology toward more realistic and practical applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper demonstrates clear novelty and makes a significant contribution to the field. The use of textured 3D guidance is a strong idea that effectively addresses temporal incoherence. The new dataset and experimental results further support the paper's claims.  While the computational complexity and reliance on external tools are limitations, they don't detract significantly from the overall value of the work.

### Towards Harnessing the Collaborative Power of Large and Small Models for Domain Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17421v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Liu, Bingjie Yan, Tianyuan Zou, Jianqing Zhang, Zixuan Gu, Jianbing Ding, Xidong Wang, Jingyi Li, Xiaozhou Ye, Ye Ouyang, Qiang Yang, Ya-Qin Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, but they require vast amounts of data and computational resources. In contrast, smaller models (SMs), while less powerful, can be more efficient and tailored to specific domains. In this position paper, we argue that taking a collaborative approach, where large and small models work synergistically, can accelerate the adaptation of LLMs to private domains and unlock new potential in AI. We explore various strategies for model collaboration and identify potential challenges and opportunities. Building upon this, we advocate for industry-driven research that prioritizes multi-objective benchmarks on real-world private datasets and applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize and critically evaluate the provided paper, offering a reasoned score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Towards Harnessing the Collaborative Power of Large and Small Models for Domain Tasks" advocates for a collaborative AI paradigm where large language models (LLMs) and smaller, domain-specific models (SMs) work synergistically.  The authors argue that this collaboration is essential to overcome the limitations of relying solely on LLMs for private domain tasks, addressing concerns around data privacy, model security, resource efficiency, and deployment flexibility. The paper presents a problem formulation outlining the constraints of private domain data and limited resources, surveys existing knowledge transfer techniques between LLMs and SMs (categorizing them as knowledge transfer from LLMs to SMs, SMs to LLMs, and collaborative inference), and identifies key challenges and opportunities, including privacy threats, efficiency considerations, and the need for standardized benchmarks. The paper concludes by advocating for application-driven research with multi-objective benchmarks using real-world private datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper doesn't introduce any technically novel methods. Its primary contribution is highlighting and synthesizing the potential benefits of LLM-SM collaboration, formulating the problem, and structuring existing techniques within this framework. The categorization of knowledge transfer techniques is helpful, but not groundbreaking. While the idea of LLM-SM collaboration isn't entirely new, the paper's focus on the specific context of *private domain* tasks is a key distinction.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its advocacy for a more practical and responsible approach to AI deployment.  The challenges it addresses – data privacy, resource constraints, model security – are all highly relevant and pressing concerns within the AI community and particularly for industry adoption. The emphasis on application-driven research and multi-objective benchmarks is also valuable, as it encourages a shift towards more real-world problem-solving. However, the generality of the discussion is also a weakness.  It lacks specific case studies or detailed examples that would further strengthen the argument. The multi-objective benchmark formulation is a good starting point, but needs further refinement with concrete metrics and a clearer methodology.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly articulates the problem of adapting LLMs to private domain tasks.
    *   Provides a useful categorization of existing knowledge transfer techniques.
    *   Highlights the importance of data privacy and model security.
    *   Advocates for application-driven research with multi-objective benchmarks.
    *   Addresses the practical limitations of solely relying on large models, such as resource constraints and deployment challenges

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Lacks technical novelty.
    *   The proposed benchmark framework needs to be more concrete.
    *   The paper remains at a high level without delving into specific examples or detailed case studies of successful or failed LLM-SM collaborations.
    *   The discussion of privacy threats, while important, is somewhat generic and could benefit from a deeper analysis of specific attack vectors and defense mechanisms relevant to the LLM-SM collaboration context.
    *   The paper focuses more on advocating for the collaborative approach and less on thoroughly analyzing the limitations of existing transfer methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research by encouraging a focus on practical, real-world problems and by emphasizing the importance of addressing data privacy and resource efficiency. It serves as a useful framework for thinking about LLM-SM collaboration.

*   **Rigour of Rationale:** The paper's argumentation is logical and well-structured, effectively conveying its main message. The identification of challenges and opportunities is sound and supported by relevant references. However, a stronger validation of the significance through concrete experimental findings or detailed analyses of real-world applications would bolster the paper's conclusions.

**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-structured argument for LLM-SM collaboration in private domain tasks, addressing relevant challenges and providing a useful overview of existing techniques. However, its lack of technical novelty and specific practical examples limit its impact. While the advocacy for application-driven research and multi-objective benchmarks is commendable, the paper's influence hinges on the subsequent development and adoption of these ideas by the research community. The paper would significantly benefit from more concrete experiments and case studies to back up its claims. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects its valuable contribution in framing the problem and providing a structured overview but acknowledges its limitations in technical depth and practical validation.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Leveraging Large Language Model Summaries for Topic Modeling in Source Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17426v1)
- **Authors**: Michele Carissimi, Martina Saletta, Claudio Ferretti
- **Abstract**: Understanding source code is a topic of great interest in the software engineering community, since it can help programmers in various tasks such as software maintenance and reuse. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable program comprehension capabilities, while transformer-based topic modeling techniques offer effective ways to extract semantic information from text. This paper proposes and explores a novel approach that combines these strengths to automatically identify meaningful topics in a corpus of Python programs. Our method consists in applying topic modeling on the descriptions obtained by asking an LLM to summarize the code. To assess the internal consistency of the extracted topics, we compare them against topics inferred from function names alone, and those derived from existing docstrings. Experimental results suggest that leveraging LLM-generated summaries provides interpretable and semantically rich representation of code structure. The promising results suggest that our approach can be fruitfully applied in various software engineering tasks such as automatic documentation and tagging, code search, software reorganization and knowledge discovery in large repositories.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Leveraging Large Language Model Summaries for Topic Modeling in Source Code":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to topic modeling of source code by leveraging large language models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on traditional elements like identifiers, comments, or docstrings (which may be missing or inconsistent), the method involves: (1) Removing comments, docstrings, and obfuscating function names from Python code. (2) Using an LLM (specifically Gemma 2B-it) to generate summaries of these functions. (3) Applying topic modeling (BERTopic) to the LLM-generated summaries. The generated topics were validated quantitatively using coherence scores and qualitatively by comparing them against topics derived from docstrings and function names. The experimental results indicate that LLM-generated summaries provide interpretable and semantically rich representations of code structure, even in the absence of natural language cues.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel combination of techniques. While topic modeling of code and using LLMs for code understanding are not new individually, the approach of using LLMs to *generate summaries specifically for topic modeling* and then comparing the resulting topics to those derived from traditional elements represents an original contribution. The deliberate removal of natural language elements before summarizing is a key aspect that differentiates this work.

* **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is moderate. The ability to automatically extract meaningful topics from code, even when lacking documentation or consistent naming, could be beneficial for several software engineering tasks:
    * **Automatic Documentation and Tagging:** The extracted topics could be used to automatically generate tags or labels for code components, improving code discoverability.
    * **Code Search:**  Topic-based search could provide more relevant results than keyword-based search, especially in large code repositories.
    * **Software Reorganization and Knowledge Discovery:** Identifying latent themes can help reorganize code into more logical modules and uncover hidden relationships between code components.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly addresses the limitations of traditional topic modeling approaches for source code.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The steps involved in the proposed method are clearly described, including data preprocessing, LLM prompting, and topic modeling.
    * **Appropriate Evaluation:** The evaluation includes both quantitative measures (coherence scores) and qualitative comparison with topics derived from docstrings and function names. The use of several distance metrics (dMSE, dTOP, dTOPw, dn) to assess the alignment of topics is a plus.
    * **Reproducibility:** The availability of the code and data is a strength.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to Python code.  It is unclear if the approach would generalize well to other programming languages.
    * **LLM Dependency:** The approach is dependent on the capabilities of the chosen LLM (Gemma 2B-it). Performance and topic quality are likely to vary with different LLMs, especially on different code styles or languages. The paper offers limited discussion on this sensitivity.
    * **Lack of comparison with LLM-directly for topic modeling:** More advanced approaches could directly employ the LLM to generate topics rather than using an intermediate summarization step. Direct comparison to the baseline could clarify whether the summary generation is indeed beneficial.
    * **Coherence limitations:** The coherence score of 0.6 while better than the docstrings, only suggests moderate interpretability. More focus on strategies to improve the LLM summaries might prove worthwhile.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially useful technique. The experimental results demonstrate that LLM-generated summaries can be effectively used for topic modeling of source code, even in the absence of natural language cues. However, the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope, and the dependency on the specific LLM needs further investigation. The significance is solid, with plausible applications in software engineering.

### Beyond Whole Dialogue Modeling: Contextual Disentanglement for Conversational Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17427v1)
- **Authors**: Guojia An, Jie Zou, Jiwei Wei, Chaoning Zhang, Fuming Sun, Yang Yang
- **Abstract**: Conversational recommender systems aim to provide personalized recommendations by analyzing and utilizing contextual information related to dialogue. However, existing methods typically model the dialogue context as a whole, neglecting the inherent complexity and entanglement within the dialogue. Specifically, a dialogue comprises both focus information and background information, which mutually influence each other. Current methods tend to model these two types of information mixedly, leading to misinterpretation of users' actual needs, thereby lowering the accuracy of recommendations. To address this issue, this paper proposes a novel model to introduce contextual disentanglement for improving conversational recommender systems, named DisenCRS. The proposed model DisenCRS employs a dual disentanglement framework, including self-supervised contrastive disentanglement and counterfactual inference disentanglement, to effectively distinguish focus information and background information from the dialogue context under unsupervised conditions. Moreover, we design an adaptive prompt learning module to automatically select the most suitable prompt based on the specific dialogue context, fully leveraging the power of large language models. Experimental results on two widely used public datasets demonstrate that DisenCRS significantly outperforms existing conversational recommendation models, achieving superior performance on both item recommendation and response generation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Whole Dialogue Modeling: Contextual Disentanglement for Conversational Recommendation" addresses the limitations of existing conversational recommender systems (CRS) that model the entire dialogue context as a whole, neglecting the inherent complexity and entanglement within the dialogue, comprising both focus and background information. To address this, the paper proposes a novel model called DisenCRS, which introduces contextual disentanglement to improve conversational recommendation accuracy. DisenCRS employs a dual disentanglement framework consisting of self-supervised contrastive disentanglement and counterfactual inference disentanglement to distinguish between focus and background information without supervision.  An adaptive prompt learning module is designed to automatically select the most suitable prompt based on the specific dialogue context, leveraging the power of large language models.  Experimental results on two datasets (ReDial and INSPIRED) demonstrate that DisenCRS outperforms existing CRS models on both item recommendation and response generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of disentangling focus and background information in CRS is a meaningful contribution. While disentanglement has been explored in other areas of recommendation, its application to the dialogue context in CRSs is relatively novel. The dual disentanglement framework and adaptive prompt learning module further contribute to the novelty. The adaptive prompt learning module builds on prior prompt-learning work and adds the element of dynamic selection based on context. The most significant novelty lies in the combination of these techniques and their application to the specific challenges of conversational recommendation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the accuracy and relevance of recommendations in CRSs. By better understanding user intent through disentangled context, the model can offer more personalized and effective recommendations. Improved response generation also contributes to a more natural and engaging user experience. The experimental results support this, showing significant performance gains over strong baselines. This also contributes to a more explainable system, in that the distinction between focus and background, when visualized, might aid interpretability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of treating the dialogue context as a monolithic entity and motivates the need for disentanglement.
    *   **Well-Designed Model:** The DisenCRS model is well-structured and incorporates several innovative components, including dual disentanglement framework and adaptive prompt learning.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental evaluation is thorough and uses widely used datasets and evaluation metrics. The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of DisenCRS compared to state-of-the-art baselines.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The ablation studies and hyperparameter analysis provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component and the sensitivity of the model to different settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** While the combination is novel, the individual components (e.g., contrastive learning, counterfactual inference, prompt learning) are based on existing techniques. The paper could benefit from a deeper theoretical justification for why this particular combination is effective for CRS.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper primarily focuses on improving accuracy and fluency metrics. It lacks a discussion of how DisenCRS might impact other important factors, such as the computational cost, scalability, or fairness of the CRS.  While DialoGPT-small is used, the full model complexity is not explored, and this may limit real-world applicability.
    *   **Generalizability:** The experiments are conducted on movie recommendation datasets.  The generalizability of DisenCRS to other domains (e.g., e-commerce, travel) is not explored.  Do the focus/background factors easily translate across different tasks?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in CRSs by highlighting the importance of contextual disentanglement and prompting researchers to explore more sophisticated techniques for understanding user intent in dialogue. The proposed DisenCRS model can serve as a strong baseline for future work in this area. The dual-disentanglement framework could also be applied to other NLP tasks that involve complex contextual information.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel approach to CRS by introducing contextual disentanglement. While the individual components are not entirely new, their combination and application to CRS, combined with an adaptive prompt learning mechanism, represents a worthwhile contribution. The strong experimental results and analysis support the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, the reliance on existing techniques, the lack of discussion on practical considerations (computational cost), and the limited generalizability prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper is a solid advance in the field, but has some limitations for realizing real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Breaking the Modality Barrier: Universal Embedding Learning with Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17432v1)
- **Authors**: Tiancheng Gu, Kaicheng Yang, Ziyong Feng, Xingjun Wang, Yanzhao Zhang, Dingkun Long, Yingda Chen, Weidong Cai, Jiankang Deng
- **Abstract**: The Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) framework has become a widely used approach for multimodal representation learning, particularly in image-text retrieval and clustering. However, its efficacy is constrained by three key limitations: (1) text token truncation, (2) isolated image-text encoding, and (3) deficient compositionality due to bag-of-words behavior. While recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant advances in generalized vision-language understanding, their potential for learning transferable multimodal representations remains underexplored.In this work, we present UniME (Universal Multimodal Embedding), a novel two-stage framework that leverages MLLMs to learn discriminative representations for diverse downstream tasks. In the first stage, we perform textual discriminative knowledge distillation from a powerful LLM-based teacher model to enhance the embedding capability of the MLLM\'s language component. In the second stage, we introduce hard negative enhanced instruction tuning to further advance discriminative representation learning. Specifically, we initially mitigate false negative contamination and then sample multiple hard negatives per instance within each batch, forcing the model to focus on challenging samples. This approach not only improves discriminative power but also enhances instruction-following ability in downstream tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on the MMEB benchmark and multiple retrieval tasks, including short and long caption retrieval and compositional retrieval. Results demonstrate that UniME achieves consistent performance improvement across all tasks, exhibiting superior discriminative and compositional capabilities.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Breaking the Modality Barrier: Universal Embedding Learning with Multimodal LLMs" introduces UniME, a novel two-stage framework for learning universal multimodal embeddings using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The first stage involves textual discriminative knowledge distillation, where a powerful LLM-based teacher model enhances the embedding capability of the MLLM's language component. The second stage employs hard negative enhanced instruction tuning, which mitigates false negative contamination and samples multiple hard negatives per instance. This improves the discriminative power and instruction-following ability for downstream tasks. The authors demonstrate UniME's performance on the MMEB benchmark and multiple retrieval tasks, showing consistent improvements in discriminative and compositional capabilities. The key idea is to leverage MLLMs for learning better embeddings, as they are usually trained for next token prediction, which is not ideal.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The idea of using MLLMs for universal multimodal embeddings is interesting. While MLLMs have shown progress in vision-language understanding, their potential for learning transferable representations is underexplored.
    *   Textual discriminative knowledge distillation is well-motivated. By transferring knowledge from a powerful LLM-based teacher, the MLLM's language component benefits.
    *   Hard negative enhanced instruction tuning is also a valuable contribution. Addressing false negative contamination and sampling multiple hard negatives is expected to improve the model's ability to differentiate between challenging samples.
    *   The two-stage framework seems practical and addresses the limitations of both MLLMs and CLIP-like models.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper demonstrates strong experimental results on the MMEB benchmark and retrieval tasks, suggesting the effectiveness of UniME. These results show consistent improvements over strong baselines, which is significant.
    *   The improvements in both discriminative and compositional capabilities are compelling, as they address important limitations of existing methods.
    *   The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a new approach for learning multimodal representations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed framework with two distinct stages.
    *   Strong experimental results and ablation studies.
    *   Addressing limitations of existing approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper's novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. Knowledge distillation and hard negative mining are established techniques.
    *   The framework is complex and may require significant computational resources.
    *   The reliance on MMEB is good, but the benchmark itself may be limited in its scope.
    *   While the individual components are valuable, their integration into a cohesive framework is what offers real benefit, but perhaps warrants more scrutiny to fully articulate the nature of that integration, and the potential for future iterations.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could inspire further research on using MLLMs for multimodal representation learning.
    *   The proposed techniques could be adopted in other applications, such as retrieval augmented generation and visual question answering.
    *   The hard negative enhanced instruction tuning could be a valuable contribution to contrastive learning.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal representation learning by proposing a well-designed and effective framework for learning universal embeddings using MLLMs. While the individual components are not entirely novel, their combination and application to MLLMs are significant. The experimental results are strong, and the paper addresses important limitations of existing methods. However, the limitations described above, such as the incremental nature of the novelty and the complexity of the framework, prevent it from receiving a higher score.

### Adaptive Orchestration of Modular Generative Information Access Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17454v1)
- **Authors**: Mohanna Hoveyda, Harrie Oosterhuis, Arjen P. de Vries, Maarten de Rijke, Faegheh Hasibi
- **Abstract**: Advancements in large language models (LLMs) have driven the emergence of complex new systems to provide access to information, that we will collectively refer to as modular generative information access (GenIA) systems. They integrate a broad and evolving range of specialized components, including LLMs, retrieval models, and a heterogeneous set of sources and tools. While modularity offers flexibility, it also raises critical challenges: How can we systematically characterize the space of possible modules and their interactions? How can we automate and optimize interactions among these heterogeneous components? And, how do we enable this modular system to dynamically adapt to varying user query requirements and evolving module capabilities? In this perspective paper, we argue that the architecture of future modular generative information access systems will not just assemble powerful components, but enable a self-organizing system through real-time adaptive orchestration -- where components' interactions are dynamically configured for each user input, maximizing information relevance while minimizing computational overhead. We give provisional answers to the questions raised above with a roadmap that depicts the key principles and methods for designing such an adaptive modular system. We identify pressing challenges, and propose avenues for addressing them in the years ahead. This perspective urges the IR community to rethink modular system designs for developing adaptive, self-optimizing, and future-ready architectures that evolve alongside their rapidly advancing underlying technologies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Adaptive Orchestration of Modular Generative Information Access Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the emerging trend of modular generative information access (GenIA) systems, which combine large language models (LLMs) with specialized components for tasks like retrieval, knowledge graph querying, and tool use.  It argues that current GenIA systems often have static architectures that are not well-suited to the diverse needs of users and queries.  The authors propose a new architecture based on dynamic, adaptive orchestration of modules. This approach involves representing the system as a graph, where nodes are modules (tasks, agents, resources) and edges represent interactions. For each query, the system dynamically constructs a pipeline by selecting and connecting the most relevant modules, optimizing for a balance between effectiveness and efficiency.  The paper outlines the key challenges, proposes a general framework, and provides a proof-of-concept implementation using a contextual multi-armed bandit (CMAB) approach for a question answering task.  The experimental results demonstrate that the adaptive approach can outperform static architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamic, query-specific orchestration of modules in GenIA systems is relatively novel, though it builds on related work in multi-agent systems (e.g., Society of Minds, GPTSwarm) and mixture of experts. The emphasis on *adaptivity* as a central architectural principle distinguishes this work.  The combination of a graph-based representation with CMAB for online learning of pipeline configurations is a valuable contribution. The notion of using different contextual features of the query to decide which pipeline architecture is best suits the needs is also well thought of.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the evolving landscape of information access: how to build systems that are both powerful *and* efficient. The modular approach allows for flexible integration of new components and adaptation to changing user needs. If effectively implemented, such adaptive orchestration frameworks could significantly improve the performance, scalability, and maintainability of GenIA systems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current approaches and motivates the need for adaptive orchestration.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:**  The proposed framework provides a structured way to think about module selection, interaction, and optimization. The modular approach is also well thought of.
    *   **Proof-of-Concept Implementation:** The CMAB-based implementation provides empirical evidence that the proposed approach can be effective.
    *   **Discussion of Challenges:**  The paper acknowledges the limitations of the current implementation and outlines important directions for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Evaluation:**  The experimental setup is relatively simple and focuses on a single question answering task. It has 7 different models to be explored, and the experiments are not compared agains other frameworks such as ReAct and Autogen. More extensive evaluations across diverse tasks and datasets would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:**  The CMAB approach, while suitable for the proof-of-concept, may not scale well to systems with a much larger number of modules and possible configurations.  The paper acknowledges this, but does not provide concrete solutions or strategies for addressing scalability.
    *   **Feature Engineering Dependency:** The performance of the adaptive orchestration relies heavily on the quality of the contextual features used to represent queries. How to automatically learn or discover these features remains an open question.
    *   **Simplified Cost Model:** The paper considers only response time as part of the cost function, and the others are only mentioned briefly, but not tested in the experimentation. It is important to consider cost for other aspects such as Carbon footprint and API usage to have a better understaing of the optimal model.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could significantly influence the design of future GenIA systems by shifting the focus from static pipelines to dynamic, adaptive architectures. It can also encourage research into new optimization techniques (e.g., hierarchical reinforcement learning) and feature engineering methods tailored for adaptive orchestration.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a compelling vision for the future of GenIA systems and offers a solid framework with a proof-of-concept implementation. The ideas are innovative and addresses an important problem that has not been fully solved. However, the limitations in experimental evaluation and scalability, and the simplification of the cost model prevent it from reaching a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unified Attacks to Large Language Model Watermarks: Spoofing and Scrubbing in Unauthorized Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17480v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Yi, Shunfan Zhengc, Linlin Wanga, Xiaoling Wang, Liang He
- **Abstract**: Watermarking has emerged as a critical technique for combating misinformation and protecting intellectual property in large language models (LLMs). A recent discovery, termed watermark radioactivity, reveals that watermarks embedded in teacher models can be inherited by student models through knowledge distillation. On the positive side, this inheritance allows for the detection of unauthorized knowledge distillation by identifying watermark traces in student models. However, the robustness of watermarks against scrubbing attacks and their unforgeability in the face of spoofing attacks under unauthorized knowledge distillation remain largely unexplored. Existing watermark attack methods either assume access to model internals or fail to simultaneously support both scrubbing and spoofing attacks. In this work, we propose Contrastive Decoding-Guided Knowledge Distillation (CDG-KD), a unified framework that enables bidirectional attacks under unauthorized knowledge distillation. Our approach employs contrastive decoding to extract corrupted or amplified watermark texts via comparing outputs from the student model and weakly watermarked references, followed by bidirectional distillation to train new student models capable of watermark removal and watermark forgery, respectively. Extensive experiments show that CDG-KD effectively performs attacks while preserving the general performance of the distilled model. Our findings underscore critical need for developing watermarking schemes that are robust and unforgeable.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Contrastive Decoding-Guided Knowledge Distillation (CDG-KD), a unified framework for attacking watermarks in large language models (LLMs) when unauthorized knowledge distillation is used.  CDG-KD enables both watermark scrubbing (removal) and spoofing (forgery) by manipulating a student model distilled from a larger, watermarked teacher model.
