### Towards Robust LLMs: an Adversarial Robustness Measurement Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17723v1)
- **Authors**: Natan Levy, Adiel Ashrov, Guy Katz
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has revolutionized artificial intelligence, yet these models remain vulnerable to adversarial perturbations, undermining their reliability in high-stakes applications. While adversarial robustness in vision-based neural networks has been extensively studied, LLM robustness remains under-explored. We adapt the Robustness Measurement and Assessment (RoMA) framework to quantify LLM resilience against adversarial inputs without requiring access to model parameters. By comparing RoMA's estimates to those of formal verification methods, we demonstrate its accuracy with minimal error margins while maintaining computational efficiency. Our empirical evaluation reveals that robustness varies significantly not only between different models but also across categories within the same task and between various types of perturbations. This non-uniformity underscores the need for task-specific robustness evaluations, enabling practitioners to compare and select models based on application-specific robustness requirements. Our work provides a systematic methodology to assess LLM robustness, advancing the development of more reliable language models for real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for assessing the adversarial robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) called ROMA (Robustness Measurement and Assessment). It adapts the ROMA framework, originally designed for image classification DNNs, to quantify the resilience of LLMs against adversarial inputs without needing access to the model's internal parameters (black-box setting). The method utilizes semantic perturbations generated using word embeddings and analyzes the distribution of confidence scores to estimate robustness.  The paper provides empirical evidence by comparing RoMA's estimates to exact verification methods on smaller models and then applies it to larger LLMs, evaluating robustness against embedding sensitivity, categorial variations (positive vs. negative sentiment), and typographical errors. The results show that robustness varies significantly across models, categories, and perturbation types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core contribution of the paper is the adaptation and application of the existing ROMA framework to the challenging domain of LLMs. While the underlying ROMA methodology itself isn't new, its application to LLMs is novel and addresses a significant gap in robustness evaluation for these powerful models.  The paper's specific techniques for generating semantically meaningful perturbations in the embedding space and applying the ROMA analysis in this context also represent a valuable contribution. However, the paper doesn't propose fundamentally new methods for robustness measurement itself.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical and timely problem.  LLMs are increasingly used in high-stakes applications where reliability and robustness are paramount. The demonstrated vulnerability of LLMs to adversarial attacks necessitates the development of robust evaluation methodologies. The ROMA framework offers several advantages:

*   **Scalability:** It offers a scalable alternative to formal verification methods, which are computationally intractable for LLMs.
*   **Black-box accessibility:** ROMA doesn't require access to the model's internal parameters, making it applicable to proprietary or black-box models.
*   **Practicality:** It provides a systematic and practical way to assess LLM robustness against various types of real-world perturbations.

The findings regarding the non-uniformity of robustness across models, categories, and perturbation types are also significant. This highlights the importance of task-specific and perturbation-aware robustness evaluations.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear and well-structured presentation.
*   Solid empirical validation against formal verification methods.
*   Comprehensive evaluation of LLM robustness across different perturbation types.
*   Practical and scalable methodology applicable to black-box LLMs.
*   Addresses a timely and important problem in AI safety.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Relies on an existing robustness measurement technique (ROMA). The core contribution lies in the *application* of ROMA to LLMs, not the *invention* of a new methodology.
*   The performance and reliability of RoMA depends on the quality of the word embeddings used and assumes that those embeddings capture the semantic similarity accurately.
*   While the paper demonstrates robustness estimates, it doesn't provide concrete methods for *improving* the robustness of LLMs. It is primarily focused on *evaluating* robustness.
*   The generalizability of the findings may be limited by the specific datasets and LLMs used in the experiments.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM robustness. It provides a valuable methodology for practitioners to assess the robustness of their models and for researchers to develop more robust LLMs. The insights into the non-uniformity of robustness can guide the development of more targeted robustness defenses.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by adapting and applying an existing robustness measurement framework to LLMs. It offers a practical and scalable way to evaluate LLM robustness and provides valuable insights into the vulnerabilities of these models. However, it does not introduce fundamentally new robustness measurement techniques and focuses primarily on evaluation rather than mitigation. Given these considerations, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conversational Assistants to support Heart Failure Patients: comparing a Neurosymbolic Architecture with ChatGPT
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17753v1)
- **Authors**: Anuja Tayal, Devika Salunke, Barbara Di Eugenio, Paula Allen-Meares, Eulalia Puig Abril, Olga Garcia, Carolyn Dickens, Andrew Boyd
- **Abstract**: Conversational assistants are becoming more and more popular, including in healthcare, partly because of the availability and capabilities of Large Language Models. There is a need for controlled, probing evaluations with real stakeholders which can highlight advantages and disadvantages of more traditional architectures and those based on generative AI. We present a within-group user study to compare two versions of a conversational assistant that allows heart failure patients to ask about salt content in food. One version of the system was developed in-house with a neurosymbolic architecture, and one is based on ChatGPT. The evaluation shows that the in-house system is more accurate, completes more tasks and is less verbose than the one based on ChatGPT; on the other hand, the one based on ChatGPT makes fewer speech errors and requires fewer clarifications to complete the task. Patients show no preference for one over the other.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a within-group user study comparing two conversational assistants designed to help heart failure patients manage their salt intake by providing information on the salt content of foods. One system (HFFood-NS) utilizes a neuro-symbolic architecture, while the other (HFFood-GPT) is based on ChatGPT. The study involved African-American patients hospitalized for heart failure. The evaluation considered both intrinsic (task performance metrics like accuracy, completion rate, and speech error rate) and extrinsic (user perception surveys). The results showed that the neuro-symbolic system was more accurate, complete more tasks, and less verbose. The ChatGPT-based system made fewer speech errors and required fewer clarifications. However, users showed no strong preference for either system. The paper discusses the challenges of evaluating LLM-based dialogue systems in healthcare and emphasizes the importance of user studies with real stakeholders.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the comparison of a traditional neuro-symbolic approach with a LLM-based approach *in a real-world healthcare context*. The use of a within-group study with real patients, rather than Mechanical Turk workers or student, is also a strength. However, *neither the neuro-symbolic architecture nor the use of ChatGPT for conversational assistants is, in and of itself, novel*. The application of these methods to the specific task of helping heart failure patients manage salt intake is a contribution, but not a groundbreaking one.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from several aspects:

    *   **Addressing a critical need:** Heart failure management, especially among African-American patients, is a crucial healthcare issue, and managing salt intake is a key component.
    *   **Comparing different architectural approaches:** The comparison of neuro-symbolic and LLM-based methods offers valuable insights into the trade-offs between accuracy, control, and user experience. This is important for guiding future development efforts.
    *   **Emphasizing user-centered evaluation:** The paper correctly highlights the limitations of automatic evaluation metrics for LLM-based dialogue systems and argues for the importance of incorporating user perspectives.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper surfaces important insights concerning the limitations of ChatGPT for medical applications - in particular, its inconsistency. It also reveals the importance of being very careful in how salt content is conveyed to patients - that it should be conveyed using familiar units such as pinches, not only formal units such as milligrams.
    *   **Limitations of Study:** The user base is small, and the study occurs only while they are hospitalized which limits their ability to answer specific questions since they are in a restricted diet. Also, in the introduction, the authors point out African Americans often have issues with accessing health food, but this aspect is not addressed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Rigorous methodology: The within-group design and the use of both intrinsic and extrinsic evaluation measures provide a comprehensive assessment.
    *   Focus on a relevant clinical problem: The study addresses a real need in heart failure management.
    *   Inclusion of a vulnerable population: The study focuses on African-American patients, who are disproportionately affected by heart failure.
    *   Clear presentation of results: The paper clearly presents the findings and discusses the implications for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited generalizability: The sample size is relatively small (20 patients) which affects external validity.
    *   ChatGPT's black box: It is not known which data HFFood-GPT actually accesses or the process it follows to calculate its final answers.
    *   Lack of statistical significance for user preference: The lack of a statistically significant preference between the two systems diminishes the strength of the conclusions. This is partially mitigated by the qualitative data from the surveys.
    *   Lack of generalisation on why users did not prefer ChatGPT: This is important since in medical contexts, many may not wish to use a tool that does not convey trust, even if it provides easier conversational flow.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:

    *   Encouraging further research comparing different architectures for healthcare dialogue systems.
    *   Promoting the use of user-centered evaluation methods in the development of these systems.
    *   Highlighting the importance of addressing specific needs of vulnerable populations.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, the paper receives a score of **6**. While the paper addresses an important problem and uses a rigorous methodology, the limited novelty, lack of statistically significant user preference, limited size and reliance on single type of population detract from the overall impact. The paper provides valuable insights and encourages user-centered evaluation, but it falls short of being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The work provides a useful example and valuable data which are not sufficient for a higher grade.

Score: 6
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Replay to Remember: Retaining Domain Knowledge in Streaming Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17780v1)
- **Authors**: Sneh Pillai
- **Abstract**: Continual learning in large language models (LLMs) typically encounters the critical challenge of catastrophic forgetting, where previously acquired knowledge deteriorates upon exposure to new data. While techniques like replay buffers and parameter-efficient tuning (e.g., Low-Rank Adaptation or LoRA) have been proposed, few studies investigate real-time domain adaptation under strict computational and data-stream constraints. In this paper, we demonstrate a lightweight method combining LoRA and a minimal replay mechanism in a realistic streaming setting across three diverse knowledge domains: medical question answering, genetics, and law. Using perplexity, semantic similarity, and GPT-based human-like evaluation metrics, we quantify the model's adaptation, forgetting, and recovery over time. Our experiments reveal that while catastrophic forgetting naturally occurs, even minimal replay significantly stabilizes and partially restores domain-specific knowledge. This study contributes practical insights for deploying adaptable LLMs in resource-constrained, real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates continual learning in large language models (LLMs), specifically addressing the problem of catastrophic forgetting in a real-time, resource-constrained streaming setting. The authors combine Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with a minimal replay mechanism to mitigate forgetting across three domains: medical question answering, genetics, and law. They use perplexity, semantic similarity, and GPT-based human-like evaluation metrics to quantify adaptation, forgetting, and recovery. The results show that even a minimal replay buffer significantly stabilizes and restores domain-specific knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its practical application of existing techniques (LoRA and replay) to a realistic, resource-constrained streaming scenario and a multi-domain setup. While LoRA and replay buffers are individually well-established, the combined application with a focus on real-time constraints and domain adaptation in diverse knowledge areas provides a valuable contribution. However, the techniques themselves aren't groundbreaking.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its demonstration of a lightweight, effective approach to continual learning in LLMs under conditions that closely resemble real-world deployments. The finding that even a minimal replay buffer can substantially improve knowledge retention is valuable for practitioners who need to adapt LLMs to evolving data streams without extensive retraining or computational resources. This has implications for edge computing, personalized AI agents, and scenarios requiring rapid adaptation to new information. The use of diverse evaluation metrics, including human-aligned GPT-4 assessments, is a strength.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper addresses a real-world challenge in LLM deployment.
    *   **Resource-Constrained Setting:** The experiments simulate realistic computational and data stream constraints.
    *   **Multi-Domain Evaluation:** The evaluation across diverse knowledge domains (medical, genetics, law) enhances the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Metrics:** The combination of perplexity, semantic similarity, and GPT-based evaluations provides a holistic view of model performance.
    *   Clear and concise writing

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The techniques used (LoRA and replay) are not novel in themselves. The contribution is in the application and experimental setting.
    *   **Limited Scope of Replay Mechanism:** The replay mechanism is quite basic (fixed size, no prioritized sampling). Exploring more advanced replay strategies could yield greater improvements.
    *   **Small Dataset Size/ Number of Prompts:** The number of prompts used for evaluation is not specified clearly. More rigorous validation could be performed using additional prompts and edge cases.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence practitioners working on deploying adaptable LLMs in resource-constrained environments. The findings provide concrete evidence that minimal replay mechanisms can be effective in mitigating catastrophic forgetting, even when combined with parameter-efficient tuning techniques. The demonstration of domain-specific variations in forgetting and recovery highlights the need for tailored strategies in different application areas.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper does not present a revolutionary new technique, it offers a valuable and practical contribution to the field of continual learning in LLMs. The application of LoRA and a minimal replay mechanism in a realistic streaming setting, coupled with a comprehensive evaluation framework, provides actionable insights for practitioners. The paper addresses a significant challenge in LLM deployment and demonstrates a cost-effective approach to mitigating catastrophic forgetting. The main limitation is the incremental nature of its technical novelty, as it primarily combines and applies existing methods.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Token-Shuffle: Towards High-Resolution Image Generation with Autoregressive Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17789v1)
- **Authors**: Xu Ma, Peize Sun, Haoyu Ma, Hao Tang, Chih-Yao Ma, Jialiang Wang, Kunpeng Li, Xiaoliang Dai, Yujun Shi, Xuan Ju, Yushi Hu, Artsiom Sanakoyeu, Felix Juefei-Xu, Ji Hou, Junjiao Tian, Tao Xu, Tingbo Hou, Yen-Cheng Liu, Zecheng He, Zijian He, Matt Feiszli, Peizhao Zhang, Peter Vajda, Sam Tsai, Yun Fu
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive (AR) models, long dominant in language generation, are increasingly applied to image synthesis but are often considered less competitive than Diffusion-based models. A primary limitation is the substantial number of image tokens required for AR models, which constrains both training and inference efficiency, as well as image resolution. To address this, we present Token-Shuffle, a novel yet simple method that reduces the number of image tokens in Transformer. Our key insight is the dimensional redundancy of visual vocabularies in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), where low-dimensional visual codes from visual encoder are directly mapped to high-dimensional language vocabularies. Leveraging this, we consider two key operations: token-shuffle, which merges spatially local tokens along channel dimension to decrease the input token number, and token-unshuffle, which untangles the inferred tokens after Transformer blocks to restore the spatial arrangement for output. Jointly training with textual prompts, our strategy requires no additional pretrained text-encoder and enables MLLMs to support extremely high-resolution image synthesis in a unified next-token prediction way while maintaining efficient training and inference. For the first time, we push the boundary of AR text-to-image generation to a resolution of 2048x2048 with gratifying generation performance. In GenAI-benchmark, our 2.7B model achieves 0.77 overall score on hard prompts, outperforming AR models LlamaGen by 0.18 and diffusion models LDM by 0.15. Exhaustive large-scale human evaluations also demonstrate our prominent image generation ability in terms of text-alignment, visual flaw, and visual appearance. We hope that Token-Shuffle can serve as a foundational design for efficient high-resolution image generation within MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Token-Shuffle: Towards High-Resolution Image Generation with Autoregressive Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Token-Shuffle," a novel method aimed at improving the efficiency and scalability of autoregressive (AR) models for high-resolution image generation. The core idea revolves around exploiting dimensional redundancy in visual vocabularies used by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Token-Shuffle merges spatially local tokens along the channel dimension, reducing the overall number of tokens required for Transformer computation. This is followed by a "token-unshuffle" operation after the Transformer blocks to restore the spatial arrangement for image output. The method is presented as a plug-and-play solution that doesn't necessitate pre-trained text encoders and enables high-resolution image synthesis (up to 2048x2048) in a unified next-token prediction framework. Experimental results show improvements over other AR models and even some diffusion models on certain benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of exploiting dimensional redundancy in visual vocabularies is a decent starting point, and the implementation of token-shuffle and un-shuffle is relatively simple and effective. This makes it easy to implement in practice. But the idea of token reduction has already been explored by previous literature such as using a separate image encoder to reduce the token numbers, and they may perform better. The token-shuffle is also inspired by pixel-shuffle which is widely used and does not contribute much to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's claim of achieving state-of-the-art performance for AR-based image generation at high resolutions is a significant contribution. The ability to generate 2048x2048 images with a relatively small model (2.7B parameters) is impressive and addresses a key limitation of AR models compared to diffusion models. But performance of AR based T2I models are still not comparable with Diffusion based models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity:** The Token-Shuffle method is conceptually simple and easy to implement, making it accessible to other researchers.
    *   **Efficiency:** The method demonstrably reduces computational cost, allowing for higher-resolution image generation.
    *   **Strong Results:** Quantitative and qualitative results suggest improved performance compared to existing AR models and even surpassing some diffusion models.
    *   **Plug-and-Play Nature:** The method's design as a plug-and-play module is a definite advantage, allowing it to be easily integrated into existing MLLM architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While ablation studies are presented, a more comprehensive exploration of different shuffle window sizes and architectural choices would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Dependence on VQGAN:** The method still relies on VQGAN for tokenization, which introduces its own limitations and artifacts.
    *   **Lack of In-depth Analysis:** There is a deficiency of analyses on how Token-Shuffle is performing, whether the training is stable and on how to optimize it further.
    *   **Questionable Comparisons:** The comparisons made with other autoregressive and diffusion methods are not entirely on equal footing (e.g., different parameter counts, training data, and hardware).
    *   **Visual Flaws:** The paper acknowledges the existence of visual flaws that is common in AR models, but there are limited explorations on how to mitigate the problem.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the reported performance gains are reproducible, Token-Shuffle has the potential to significantly impact the field of image generation. It could lead to more efficient and scalable MLLMs capable of generating high-resolution images, enabling new applications in areas like content creation, virtual reality, and scientific visualization.

*   **Justification for Score:** Token-Shuffle represents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the efficiency bottleneck in AR-based image generation. While the novelty is relatively small (inspired by pixel-shuffle), the simplicity, ease of implementation and demonstrated efficiency gains make it a solid technique to further explore.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### LiDPM: Rethinking Point Diffusion for Lidar Scene Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17791v1)
- **Authors**: Tetiana Martyniuk, Gilles Puy, Alexandre Boulch, Renaud Marlet, Raoul de Charette
- **Abstract**: Training diffusion models that work directly on lidar points at the scale of outdoor scenes is challenging due to the difficulty of generating fine-grained details from white noise over a broad field of view. The latest works addressing scene completion with diffusion models tackle this problem by reformulating the original DDPM as a local diffusion process. It contrasts with the common practice of operating at the level of objects, where vanilla DDPMs are currently used. In this work, we close the gap between these two lines of work. We identify approximations in the local diffusion formulation, show that they are not required to operate at the scene level, and that a vanilla DDPM with a well-chosen starting point is enough for completion. Finally, we demonstrate that our method, LiDPM, leads to better results in scene completion on SemanticKITTI. The project page is https://astra-vision.github.io/LiDPM .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LiDPM paper:

**Summary**

The paper "LiDPM: Rethinking Point Diffusion for Lidar Scene Completion" addresses the problem of completing lidar scans for autonomous driving.  It critiques recent work (LiDiff) that reformulated Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs) into a "local diffusion" process to handle large-scale automotive scenes.  LiDPM argues that this local diffusion formulation is unnecessary and introduces approximations. The paper demonstrates that a vanilla DDPM, with appropriate conditioning and architectural choices (specifically, instance normalization), can effectively perform lidar scene completion on a global scale.  The authors achieve better results on the SemanticKITTI dataset compared to LiDiff. Crucially, LiDPM can also perform scene generation, a capability that LiDiff's local approach lacked.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in demonstrating that a vanilla DDPM can effectively solve lidar scene completion without resorting to the local diffusion approximations introduced by LiDiff. This challenges the prevailing belief that local diffusion is necessary for scaling diffusion models to large point clouds.  The instance normalization approach to stabilizing training with varying conditioning inputs is also a valuable technical contribution. The ability to extend this work to both scene completion and generation further enhances the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper has a notable significance because it simplifies the diffusion framework for lidar scene completion and generation. By showing that a standard DDPM can work well, it makes the approach more accessible and potentially easier to build upon.  The improved performance on SemanticKITTI and the added capability of scene generation demonstrate the practical benefits of LiDPM. Reverting to a global diffusion formulation eliminates the need for specialized regularization terms and opens the doors for future advancements using established DDPM techniques.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing local diffusion approaches and convincingly argues for a return to a global diffusion strategy.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on SemanticKITTI demonstrate the superiority of LiDPM over LiDiff, both with and without refinement. The inclusion of a qualitative comparison further strengthens the findings.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The paper includes detailed ablation studies to analyze the impact of different design choices, such as the starting point of the diffusion process (t0) and the number of DPM-Solver steps.
    *   **Comprehensive Appendix:** The Appendix provides a thorough explanation of the local diffusion formulation and clarifies the approximations it introduces, which helps the reader understand the motivation behind LiDPM.
    *   **Well-written and Easy to Understand:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making it easy for readers to understand the technical details and the key contributions.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LiDiff's Architecture:** LiDPM uses the same architecture as LiDiff (MinkUNet) with a change to instance normalization. While this allows for a fair comparison, it limits the exploration of potentially more powerful or efficient architectures.  The core improvement comes not from architectural innovation but from the diffusion process.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the SemanticKITTI results are compelling, the paper could benefit from evaluating LiDPM on other lidar datasets or in the context of downstream tasks (e.g., object detection, segmentation).
    *   **Incremental improvement**: While LiDPM improves the state-of-the-art, the improvement isn't drastically revolutionary.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to have a positive impact on the field of lidar scene completion and generation. The simplified diffusion framework and the improved performance make LiDPM a promising approach for future research and applications. The clarification of the local vs. global diffusion trade-offs will also be valuable for researchers working on other point cloud diffusion tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign the following score:

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed contribution to the field. It effectively refutes the necessity of local diffusion for lidar scene completion, simplifies the diffusion framework, and demonstrates improved performance. The use of instance normalization is a critical insight. However, the reliance on an existing architecture, the incremental nature of the improvements, and the limited scope of evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. While important and potentially impactful, the paper builds directly on prior work without introducing radical new concepts or techniques. A score of 7 reflects the balance between the valuable contributions and the aforementioned limitations.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
